He had managed to get close to the presidential podium, but did not fire, uncertain of hitting his target.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMiller298–300_206-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller298–300-206">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> After hearing a speech by anarchist <a href="/wiki/Emma_Goldman" title="Emma Goldman">Emma Goldman</a> in Cleveland, Czolgosz had decided to take action that he believed would advance the cause. After his failure to get close enough on September 5, Czolgosz waited until the next day at the <a href="/wiki/Temple_of_Music" title="Temple of Music">Temple of Music</a> on the exposition grounds, where the president was to meet the public. Czolgosz concealed his gun in a handkerchief and, when he reached the head of the line, shot McKinley twice in the abdomen at close range.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMiller300–01_208-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller300–01-208">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>McKinley urged his aides to break the news gently to Ida, and to call off the mob that had set upon Czolgosz, a request that may have saved his assassin's life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMiller301–02_209-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller301–02-209">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> McKinley was taken to the exposition aid station, where the doctor was unable to locate the second bullet. Although a primitive <a href="/wiki/X-ray_machine" title="X-ray machine">X-ray machine</a> was being exhibited on the exposition grounds, it was not used. McKinley was taken to the home of <a href="/wiki/John_G._Milburn" title="John G. Milburn">John G. Milburn</a>, president of the Pan-American Exposition Company.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeech596–97Miller312–15_210-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeech596–97Miller312–15-210">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the days after the shooting, McKinley appeared to improve and doctors issued increasingly optimistic bulletins. Members of the Cabinet, who had rushed to Buffalo on hearing the news, dispersed, and Vice President Roosevelt departed on a camping trip to the <a href="/wiki/Adirondacks" class="mw-redirect" title="Adirondacks">Adirondacks</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMiller315–17Morgan401–02_211-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller315–17Morgan401–02-211">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Leech wrote: 
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>It is difficult to interpret the optimism with which the President's physicians looked for his recovery. There was obviously the most serious danger that his wounds would become septic. In that case, he would almost certainly die, since drugs to control infection did not exist&#160;... [Prominent New York City physician] <a href="/wiki/Charles_McBurney_(surgeon)" title="Charles McBurney (surgeon)">Dr. McBurney</a> was by far the worst offender in showering sanguine assurances on the correspondents. As the only big-city surgeon on the case, he was eagerly questioned and quoted, and his rosy prognostications largely contributed to the delusion of the American public.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeech599_212-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeech599-212">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup>
</p></blockquote>
<p>On the morning of September 13, McKinley's condition deteriorated. Specialists were summoned; although at first some doctors hoped that McKinley might survive with a weakened heart, by afternoon they knew that the case was hopeless. Unknown to the doctors, <a href="/wiki/Gangrene" title="Gangrene">gangrene</a> was growing on the walls of McKinley's stomach and slowly poisoning his blood. McKinley drifted in and out of consciousness all day, but when awake he was a model patient. By evening, McKinley too knew he was dying, "It is useless, gentlemen. I think we ought to have prayer."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeech600_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeech600-213">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMiller318–319_214-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller318–319-214">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> Relatives and friends gathered around the death bed. The first lady sobbed over him, saying, "I want to go, too. I want to go, too."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMiller319–320_215-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller319–320-215">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup> Her husband replied, "We are all going, we are all going. God's will be done, not ours", and with final strength put an arm around her.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMiller320_216-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller320-216">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> He may also have sung part of his favorite hymn, "<a href="/wiki/Nearer,_My_God,_to_Thee" title="Nearer, My God, to Thee">Nearer, My God, to Thee</a>",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeech601_217-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeech601-217">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> although some other accounts have the first lady singing it softly to him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMiller320_216-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller320-216">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At 2:15&#160;a.m. on September 14, 1901, McKinley died. Theodore Roosevelt rushed back to Buffalo and took the oath of office as president. Czolgosz, put on trial for murder nine days after McKinley's death, was found guilty, sentenced to death on September 26 and executed by <a href="/wiki/Electric_chair" title="Electric chair">electric chair</a> on October 29, 1901.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMiller321–30_218-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller321–30-218">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Funeral.2C_memorials.2C_and_legacy"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Funeral,_memorials,_and_legacy">Funeral, memorials, and legacy</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Funeral_and_resting_place">Funeral and resting place</span></h3>
<p>According to Gould, "The nation experienced a wave of genuine grief at the news of McKinley's passing."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGould252_219-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGould252-219">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> The stock market, faced with sudden uncertainty, suffered a steep decline that went nearly unnoticed in the mourning. The nation focused its attention on the casket that first lay in the <a href="/wiki/East_Room" title="East Room">East Room</a> of the Executive Mansion and then laid <a href="/wiki/Lying_in_state#United_States" title="Lying in state">in state</a> in the Capitol before being transported to Canton by train.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorgan402–03_220-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorgan402–03-220">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup> Approximately 100,000 people passed by the open casket in the <a href="/wiki/Capitol_Rotunda" class="mw-redirect" title="Capitol Rotunda">Capitol Rotunda</a>, many having waited hours in the rain. In Canton, an equal number did the same at the Stark County Courthouse on September 18. The following day, a funeral service was held at the First Methodist Church. The casket was next sealed and taken to the McKinley house, where relatives paid their final respects.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcElroy167_221-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcElroy167-221">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup> It was then transported to the receiving vault at <a href="/wiki/West_Lawn_Cemetery" title="West Lawn Cemetery">West Lawn Cemetery</a> in Canton to await the construction of the memorial to McKinley already being planned.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorgan403_222-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorgan403-222">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There was a widespread expectation that Ida McKinley would not long survive her husband; one family friend stated, as William McKinley lay dying, that they should be prepared for a double funeral.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMiller348_223-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller348-223">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> However, this did not occur, and the former first lady accompanied her husband on the funeral train. Leech noted "the circuitous journey was a cruel ordeal for the woman who huddled in a compartment of the funeral train, praying that the Lord would take her with her Dearest Love."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeech602_224-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeech602-224">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup> She was thought too weak to attend the services in Washington or Canton, although she listened at the door to the service for her husband in her house on North Market Street. She remained in Canton for the remainder of her life, setting up a shrine in her house and often visiting the receiving vault, until her death at age 59 on May 26, 1907.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMiller348_223-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller348-223">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> She died only months before the completion of <a href="/wiki/McKinley_National_Memorial" title="McKinley National Memorial">the large marble monument</a> to her husband in Canton, which was dedicated by President Roosevelt on September 30, 1907. William and Ida McKinley are interred there with their daughters atop a hillside overlooking the city of Canton.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcElroy189–93Morgan406_225-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcElroy189–93Morgan406-225">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_memorials">Other memorials</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:McKinley_Birthplace_Memorial_uncirculated_dollar_(common_obverse).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/McKinley_Birthplace_Memorial_uncirculated_dollar_%28common_obverse%29.jpg/220px-McKinley_Birthplace_Memorial_uncirculated_dollar_%28common_obverse%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="220" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/McKinley_Birthplace_Memorial_uncirculated_dollar_%28common_obverse%29.jpg/330px-McKinley_Birthplace_Memorial_uncirculated_dollar_%28common_obverse%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/McKinley_Birthplace_Memorial_uncirculated_dollar_%28common_obverse%29.jpg/440px-McKinley_Birthplace_Memorial_uncirculated_dollar_%28common_obverse%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2864" data-file-height="2864" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/McKinley_Birthplace_Memorial_gold_dollar" title="McKinley Birthplace Memorial gold dollar">McKinley Birthplace Memorial gold dollar</a> was minted in 1916 and 1917</figcaption></figure>
<p>In addition to the Canton site, many other memorials honor McKinley. The <a href="/wiki/William_McKinley_Monument" title="William McKinley Monument">William McKinley Monument</a> stands in front of the <a href="/wiki/Ohio_Statehouse" title="Ohio Statehouse">Ohio Statehouse</a> in <a href="/wiki/Columbus,_Ohio" title="Columbus, Ohio">Columbus</a> and a large marble statue of McKinley is situated at <a href="/wiki/National_McKinley_Birthplace_Memorial" title="National McKinley Birthplace Memorial">his birthplace</a> in <a href="/wiki/Niles,_Ohio" title="Niles, Ohio">Niles</a>. Twenty Ohio schools bear McKinley's name,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcElroy189_226-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcElroy189-226">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup> and several more schools in the United States are named <a href="/wiki/McKinley_School_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="McKinley School (disambiguation)">McKinley School</a>. Nearly a million dollars was pledged by contributors or allocated from public funds for the construction of McKinley memorials in the year after his death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOlcott388_227-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOlcott388-227">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> McKinley biographer <a href="/wiki/Kevin_Phillips_(political_commentator)" title="Kevin Phillips (political commentator)">Kevin Phillips</a> suggests that the significant number of major memorials to McKinley in Ohio reflect the expectation among Ohioans in the years after McKinley's death that he would be ranked among the great presidents.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhillips161_228-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhillips161-228">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Statues bearing McKinley's image may be found in more than a dozen states, and his name has been bestowed on streets, civic organizations and libraries. In 1896, a gold prospector gave McKinley's name to <a href="/wiki/Denali" title="Denali">Denali</a>, the tallest mountain in North America at 20,310 feet (6,190&#160;m). The Alaska Board of Geographic Names reverted the name of the mountain to Denali, its local appellation, in 1975. The <a href="/wiki/Department_of_the_Interior" class="mw-redirect" title="Department of the Interior">Department of the Interior</a> followed suit in August 2015 as a part of a visit to Alaska by President <a href="/wiki/Barack_Obama" title="Barack Obama">Barack Obama</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-229" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-229">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> Similarly, <a href="/wiki/Denali_National_Park" class="mw-redirect" title="Denali National Park">Denali National Park</a> was known as Mount McKinley National Park until December 2, 1980, when it was changed by legislation signed by President <a href="/wiki/Jimmy_Carter" title="Jimmy Carter">Jimmy Carter</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcElroy189_226-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcElroy189-226">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy_and_historical_image">Legacy and historical image</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Harriet_Anderson_Stubbs_Murphy_-_William_McKinley_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Harriet_Anderson_Stubbs_Murphy_-_William_McKinley_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/220px-Harriet_Anderson_Stubbs_Murphy_-_William_McKinley_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="377" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Harriet_Anderson_Stubbs_Murphy_-_William_McKinley_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/330px-Harriet_Anderson_Stubbs_Murphy_-_William_McKinley_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Harriet_Anderson_Stubbs_Murphy_-_William_McKinley_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/440px-Harriet_Anderson_Stubbs_Murphy_-_William_McKinley_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3203" data-file-height="5490" /></a><figcaption>The official Presidential portrait of William McKinley, by <a href="/wiki/Harriet_Anderson_Stubbs_Murphy" title="Harriet Anderson Stubbs Murphy">Harriet Anderson Stubbs Murphy</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>McKinley's biographer H. Wayne Morgan remarks that McKinley died the most beloved president in history.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorgan404_230-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorgan404-230">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup> However, the young, enthusiastic Roosevelt quickly captured public attention. The new president made little effort to secure the trade reciprocity that McKinley had intended to negotiate with other nations. Controversy and public interest surrounded Roosevelt throughout the seven and a half years of his presidency as memories of McKinley faded; by 1920, according to Gould, McKinley's administration was deemed no more than "a mediocre prelude to the vigor and energy of Theodore Roosevelt's."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGould252_219-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGould252-219">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> Beginning in the 1950s, McKinley received more favorable evaluations; nevertheless, in surveys ranking American presidents, he has generally been placed near the middle, often trailing contemporaries such as Hayes and Cleveland.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGould252_219-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGould252-219">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> Morgan suggests that this relatively low ranking is the result of a perception among historians that while many decisions during McKinley's presidency profoundly affected the nation's future, he more followed public opinion than led it, and that McKinley's standing has suffered from altered public expectations of the presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorgan472_231-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorgan472-231">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There has been broad agreement among historians that McKinley's election occurred at a time of a transition between two political eras, dubbed the <a href="/wiki/Third_Party_System" title="Third Party System">Third</a> and <a href="/wiki/Fourth_Party_System" title="Fourth Party System">Fourth Party Systems</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENice448_232-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENice448-232">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> Kenneth F. Warren emphasizes the national commitment to a pro-business, industrial, and modernizing program represented by McKinley.<sup id="cite_ref-233" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-233">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> Historian Daniel P. Klinghard argued that McKinley's personal control of the 1896 campaign gave him the opportunity to reshape the presidency—rather than simply follow the party platform—by representing himself as the voice of the people.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlinghard736–60_234-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlinghard736–60-234">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup> Republican <a href="/wiki/Karl_Rove" title="Karl Rove">Karl Rove</a> exalted McKinley as the model for a sweeping political realignment behind George W. Bush in the 2000s<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERove_235-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERove-235">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup>—a realignment that did not happen. Historian Michael J. Korzi argued in 2005 that while it is tempting to see McKinley as the key figure in the transition from congressional domination of government to the modern, powerful president, this change was an incremental process through the late 19th and early 20th centuries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKorzi281_236-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKorzi281-236">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Phillips writes that McKinley's low rating is undeserved, and that he should be ranked just after the great presidents such as Washington and Lincoln. He pointed to McKinley's success at building an electoral coalition that kept the Republicans mostly in power for a generation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhillips156–57_237-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhillips156–57-237">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup> Phillips believes that part of McKinley's legacy is the men whom he included in his administration who dominated the Republican Party for a quarter century after his death. These officials included Cortelyou, who served in three Cabinet positions under Roosevelt, and Dawes, who became vice president under <a href="/wiki/Calvin_Coolidge" title="Calvin Coolidge">Coolidge</a>. Other McKinley appointees who later became major figures include Day, whom Roosevelt elevated to the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States" title="Supreme Court of the United States">Supreme Court</a> where he remained nearly 20 years, and <a href="/wiki/William_Howard_Taft" title="William Howard Taft">William Howard Taft</a>, whom McKinley had made <a href="/wiki/Governor-General_of_the_Philippines" title="Governor-General of the Philippines">Governor-General of the Philippines</a> and who succeeded Roosevelt as president.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhillips163–64_238-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhillips163–64-238">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup> After the assassination, the present <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secret_Service" title="United States Secret Service">United States Secret Service</a> came into existence when the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Congress" title="United States Congress">Congress</a> deemed it necessary that presidential protection be part of its duties.<sup id="cite_ref-239" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-239">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A controversial aspect of McKinley's presidency is territorial expansion and the question of imperialism; with the exception of the Philippines, granted independence in 1946, the United States retains the territories taken under McKinley.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhillips154_240-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhillips154-240">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup> The territorial expansion of 1898 is often seen by historians as the beginning of <a href="/wiki/American_imperialism" title="American imperialism">American empire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhillips99_241-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhillips99-241">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup> Morgan sees that historical discussion as a subset of the debate over the rise of America as a world power; he expects the debate over McKinley's actions to continue indefinitely without resolution, and notes that however one judges McKinley's actions in American expansion, one of his motivations was to change the lives of Filipinos and Cubans for the better.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorgan468_242-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorgan468-242">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Morgan alludes to the rise of interest in McKinley as part of the debate over the more assertive American foreign policy of recent decades:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>McKinley was a major actor in some of the most important events in American history. His decisions shaped future policies and public attitudes. He usually rises in the estimation of scholars who study his life in detail. Even those who disagree with his policies and decisions see him as an active, responsible, informed participant in charge of decision making. His dignified demeanor and subtle operations keep him somewhat remote from public perception. But he is once again at the center of events, where he started.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorgan473_243-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorgan473-243">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup>
</p></blockquote>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Explanatory_notes">Explanatory notes</span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-1"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-1">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Vice President Hobart died in office. As this was prior to the adoption of the <a href="/wiki/Twenty-fifth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution" title="Twenty-fifth Amendment to the United States Constitution">Twenty-fifth Amendment</a> in 1967, a vacancy in the office of vice president was not filled until the next ensuing election and inauguration.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Medal_of_Honor-25"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Medal_of_Honor_25-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"> In 1896, some of McKinley's comrades lobbied for him to be belatedly awarded the <a href="/wiki/Medal_of_Honor" title="Medal of Honor">Medal of Honor</a> for his bravery that day; Lieutenant General <a href="/wiki/Nelson_A._Miles" title="Nelson A. Miles">Nelson A. Miles</a> was inclined to grant McKinley the award, but when the then-President-elect heard about the effort, he declined it. See <a href="#CITEREFArmstrong">Armstrong</a>, pp.&#160;38–41; <a href="#CITEREFPhillips">Phillips</a>, p.&#160;21.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Twentieth_Amendment-53"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Twentieth_Amendment_53-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"> Until the ratification of the <a href="/wiki/Twentieth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution" title="Twentieth Amendment to the United States Constitution">20th Amendment</a> in 1933, the Constitution prescribed that Congress begin its regular sessions in early December. See <a href="#CITEREFUS_Senate,_Sessions_of_Congress">US Senate, <i>Sessions of Congress</i></a>.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Seventeenth_Amendment-75"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Seventeenth_Amendment_75-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"> Before the passage of the <a href="/wiki/Seventeenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution" title="Seventeenth Amendment to the United States Constitution">Seventeenth Amendment to the United States Constitution</a> in 1913, senators were elected by state legislatures.</span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
